From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess what really made me come in is the eating thing. Like, not just eating more than I meant to, but once I start eating like that, it feels like I hit some kind of switch and I just keep going. And the weird part is, it’s not like I’m totally unaware—there’s this part of me that’s thinking, “Dennis, just stop already, what are you doing?” But I still don’t stop.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m just making excuses or like I have no discipline, but in the moment it feels like I’m not fully in charge of it. I know I should stop, I want to stop, and then I just… keep eating anyway. That feeling is what’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Pretty much every time, honestly. Afterward I feel really embarrassed, like I don’t even want to think about what I just did, and kind of… grossed out with myself, I guess. Not even just physically, though that too, but more like, “Why couldn’t you just stop?”\n\nIt bothers me because in other parts of my life I’m usually pretty disciplined. Like with school stuff, or robotics, I can focus for hours and stick with something even if it’s frustrating. So afterward it feels really jarring, like there’s this one area where I suddenly have no control, and then I’m ashamed because it doesn’t feel like me. It makes me feel weak, I guess, even though I know that sounds harsh.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these eating episodes by doing something to compensate, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I really have a clear pattern like that. Like, I’ve definitely had the thought afterward of, “I should do something to undo this,” because I feel so awful and uncomfortable and embarrassed. But as far as actually making myself throw up or doing some kind of strict exercise thing specifically to cancel it out… I don’t know, that’s not really something I can say I do.\n\nI mean, I might feel like I should be “better” the next day or I’ll tell myself I need to get back on track, but I don’t think I’m doing a deliberate compensation thing, if that makes sense. It’s more like shame and regret than an actual plan to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… most weeks, I think. It’s not every single day or anything like that, which is part of why I kept telling myself maybe it wasn’t “serious,” but it’s been pretty regular.\n\nSince early in the semester, it’s usually like one night a week where it happens, and then some weeks it’s twice—especially if I’ve got deadlines piling up or there’s just a bunch of food around the apartment. It’s kind of embarrassing how predictable it is when I say it out loud, but yeah, I’d say that pattern has been going on steadily for at least the last three or four months.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably the main part that scared me enough to actually come in, honestly.\n\nIt’s not like I sit down and calmly decide, “I’m going to eat this much.” It feels more like once I start, some switch flips, and then I just keep going. And there’s still this part of me watching it happen, like I’m literally thinking, Dennis, stop. You’re not even enjoying this anymore. Just put it away. But then I’ll still take more, or go back again, or keep eating past the point where it makes any sense.\n\nSo yeah, it feels like I’m not really in control in those moments. Not completely. I know technically I’m the one doing it, which is part of why I feel so bad afterward, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like normal decision-making. It feels like I’m kind of locked into it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it in any exact way, and I kind of avoid the scale sometimes because I don’t want to know, if that makes sense.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but I don’t know if that’s real or just me being hyperaware after I’ve had a bad night with food. I don’t think there’s been some huge, obvious change where other people are commenting on it or anything like that. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nSo… I guess the most honest answer is I don’t really know. I haven’t measured it consistently enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… usually, yes. Or at least I try to make it look like I’m not eating that much when people are around.\n\nLike if my roommates are in the kitchen, I’ll do this thing where I eat what seems like a normal amount in front of them—something that wouldn’t make anyone look twice. I’ll kind of act casual, like I’m just making dinner or grabbing a snack. But then later I’ll take more stuff back to my room, or I’ll wait until they’re not really paying attention and go back for extra.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but a big part of it is that I don’t want them to see the full amount. I’m really aware of how it would look if someone watched the whole thing from start to finish. So even if I’m not technically alone the entire time, the worst part of it usually happens alone in my room.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these episodes of eating would still happen if you didn't feel ashamed or guilty afterward?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, actually, and I don’t know that the shame is what causes it exactly. It’s more like… the shame is what hits afterward, really hard.\n\nLike after it’s over, I’ll look at the wrappers or the dishes or whatever and just feel embarrassed and kind of grossed out with myself. Not even only physically, but mentally too, like, What was that? Why did I do that? And it bothers me a lot because in most other parts of my life I’m pretty disciplined. With school stuff, and especially robotics, I can sit with a problem for hours and be methodical and controlled and patient. I’m used to being someone who can focus and make a plan and stick to it.\n\nSo when this happens, it feels like the opposite of how I think of myself. It makes me feel like I’m not in control, and then I feel ashamed because it’s like this private thing where I don’t recognize my own behavior. I guess if I didn’t feel guilty afterward, maybe I wouldn’t be as scared by it, but I think the episodes would still feel really wrong to me because they don’t match how I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always notice it while it’s happening, but afterward it’s kind of obvious because I’ll realize the food is just… gone. Like way faster than it would be if I were eating normally.\n\nA lot of the time I’m not even sitting down properly for it. I’ll be at my desk, kind of half-standing or leaning there between homework and maybe starting a game or switching tabs or something, and I’ll just keep grabbing bites without really paying attention. It doesn’t feel like a meal where I’m tasting things and deciding if I’m full or whatever. It’s more like I’m just moving through it really fast.\n\nThen a few minutes later I’ll look down and the container or bag is empty, and I’ll have this weird moment of, Wait, did I seriously eat all of that already? So… yeah, during those times, I’d say I eat pretty quickly.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the food during these episodes, or is it more like you're not even tasting it?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m not really tasting it. I mean, I must be tasting it on some level, because I know what I’m eating, but it’s not like normal eating where I’m actually enjoying each bite or thinking, “This is good.”\n\nDuring those times, it’s just fast. Like I’ll be standing at my desk, sort of between doing homework and maybe opening a game or switching windows on my computer, and I’m just grabbing more without really registering it. It’s almost automatic. I’ll take a bite, then another, then another, and my brain is somewhere else but also weirdly locked into the food.\n\nAnd then suddenly it’s been, I don’t know, a few minutes, and the food is just gone. That’s usually when I kind of snap back and realize I barely remember eating it. So I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment. Maybe there’s a second at the beginning where it feels good, but most of it is just me eating really quickly and not paying attention until afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating because you're hungry during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention beforehand to say, like, “Yes, I was definitely hungry,” or “No, I definitely wasn’t.” In the moment I’m usually not doing that kind of check-in with myself.\n\nIt’s not usually like a normal meal where I think, Okay, I’m hungry, I should eat something. It’s more like I sort of end up eating and then keep going, and only afterward do I think about what happened. So I guess I don’t really know if hunger is the main thing there. I haven’t tracked it carefully enough to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s honestly the part that really made me come in, I think.\n\nIt’s not just that I eat too fast or that I eat more than I meant to. It feels like once I start eating in that way, something kind of flips on, like I hit a switch, and then I just keep going. And there’s usually this part of me that’s aware enough to be thinking, Dennis, just stop already. Like, put it away, close the bag, walk away, do literally anything else. But I don’t really do it.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m not completely unaware the whole time. I’ll have these little moments where I realize, Okay, I’m still eating, I should stop now, and then almost immediately I’m reaching for more anyway. It feels really frustrating because it’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Yes, I want all of this.” It feels more like I’ve already started and now stopping is suddenly way harder than it should be.\n\nSo… yeah, I do feel unable to stop once it’s going. Even if part of me wants to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight gain or loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you a clear number or say, like, “I gained this many pounds” or anything.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t feel drastically different, I don’t think. Sometimes I feel heavier or kind of uncomfortable in my body after this happens, but I’m not sure if that’s actual weight change or just me feeling bad about it in the moment. I also don’t have a routine where I check my weight, so it’s kind of hard to tell.\n\nSo… no significant gain or loss that I’ve noticed, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you lose interest in other things you normally enjoy when you're having one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. Like, during the actual eating, I’m usually pretty focused on the food and on just… continuing, I guess. So in that moment I’m not really thinking about hobbies or friends or school stuff or whatever else I normally care about.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I “lose interest” in those things. It’s more like they’re not in my head right then. I haven’t really noticed it as, like, I stop enjoying things overall or I don’t want to do anything else because of it. It’s mostly that the episode kind of takes up all the attention while it’s happening.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed a real loss of interest, no. At least not in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Pretty much every time, honestly.\n\nAfterward I get really embarrassed, even if nobody saw it happen. It’s like I’m sitting there with the wrappers or whatever, and I just feel kind of… grossed out with myself. Not even only physically, although there’s some of that, but more like, Why did I do that again? Why couldn’t I just stop? And then I start picking myself apart in my head.\n\nI think part of why it bothers me so much is that in other areas I’m usually pretty disciplined. Like with school stuff, and especially robotics, I can sit there for hours and troubleshoot something or stay organized and push through even when it’s frustrating. So then when this happens, it feels really out of character, or like there’s this one part of me that I just have no control over. And that makes me feel kind of ashamed, like I should be able to handle it better.\n\nSo yeah, I definitely criticize myself after. I try not to, but that’s usually where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide evidence of your eating during these episodes, like throwing away wrappers or cleaning up quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but maybe not always in the super obvious way of, like, immediately destroying all evidence or something. It’s more that I try to manage what people can see.\n\nLike if my roommates are around, especially if they’re in the kitchen, I’ll eat what looks like a pretty normal amount. I’ll make it seem like I’m just grabbing a snack or making a normal meal, and I’ll kind of act casual about it. But then later I’ll take extra stuff back to my room, or I’ll wait until I’m alone to get more, because I really don’t want them seeing the actual amount I’m eating.\n\nIt feels embarrassing to have someone watch that or even just notice it. I’ll think, Oh, they’re going to judge me, or they’re going to wonder why I’m eating again, or why I’m taking that much. So I end up doing this weird thing where the part people see is controlled, and then the rest happens privately.\n\nAnd yeah, sometimes I’ll clean up quickly or make sure wrappers aren’t just sitting out, but the bigger thing is honestly hiding the amount from people in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the taste of the food is pleasing to you during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Sometimes at the very beginning, maybe, but honestly during the actual episode I don’t think I’m really enjoying the taste in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m eating really fast. Like I’ll be standing at my desk or kind of hovering there between doing homework and starting a game, and I’m just putting food in my mouth without really slowing down. I’ll think I’m just having some, and then a few minutes later I look down and it’s basically gone. And I realize I barely even tasted it.\n\nSo if you asked me, “Was it good?” I could probably say yeah, it was food I like, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m savoring it or getting much pleasure from the flavor. It’s more automatic and rushed, I guess. Like the taste is almost in the background, and then afterward I’m surprised by how quickly I ate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the food tastes good to you while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… kind of, but not in the way it would if I were actually sitting down and enjoying it. Like, the food is usually stuff I like, so I’m sure it tastes good at first or on some level, but during those times I’m eating so fast that I barely register it.\n\nA lot of the time I’m standing at my desk, sort of in between things, like I’ve been doing homework and I’m about to start a game or I’m half-distracted, and I’ll just keep grabbing bites without really thinking. Then a few minutes later I look and it’s gone, and I’m like, wait, I ate all of that already? So I guess the taste is there, but it’s not really the main thing I’m noticing. It’s more rushed and automatic than actually pleasurable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance-like state or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “autopilot” is probably the right word, or at least close to it. I don’t know if I’d call it a trance exactly, like I’m completely unaware of what’s happening, because I do know I’m eating. It’s not like I black out or anything.\n\nBut once I start eating like that, it feels like some switch gets flipped. Like there’s this momentum, and I just keep going. And the frustrating part is that I’m not totally absent in my head—I’ll actually be thinking, Dennis, just stop already. Like, you don’t need more. Put it down. Walk away. And then I just… don’t. I keep reaching for more anyway.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because it scares me a little how hard it feels to interrupt it once it starts. It’s like part of me is watching it happen and trying to get control back, but the eating is already moving faster than my decision-making, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.8"], "note": "The patient's symptoms and behavior, such as eating rapidly, feeling unable to stop, and experiencing guilt and shame afterward, are more closely aligned with F50.8, which is characterized by recurrent episodes of binge eating without the compensatory behaviors seen in bulimia nervosa. The lack of clear compensatory behaviors and the primary issue being the binge eating itself suggests F50.8 as a more specific diagnosis than the broader F50.9.", "is_final": true}

I’ll have these little moments where I realize, Okay, I’m still eating, I should stop now, and then almost immediately I’m reaching for more anyway. It feels really frustrating because it’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Yes, I want all of this.” It feels more like I’ve already started and now stopping is suddenly way harder than it should be.\n\nSo… yeah, I do feel unable to stop once it’s going. Even if part of me wants to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight gain or loss over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you a clear number or say, like, “I gained this many pounds” or anything.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t feel drastically different, I don’t think. Sometimes I feel heavier or kind of uncomfortable in my body after this happens, but I’m not sure if that’s actual weight change or just me feeling bad about it in the moment. I also don’t have a routine where I check my weight, so it’s kind of hard to tell.\n\nSo… no significant gain or loss that I’ve noticed, but I’m not completely sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you lose interest in other things you normally enjoy when you're having one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. Like, during the actual eating, I’m usually pretty focused on the food and on just… continuing, I guess. So in that moment I’m not really thinking about hobbies or friends or school stuff or whatever else I normally care about.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means I “lose interest” in those things. It’s more like they’re not in my head right then. I haven’t really noticed it as, like, I stop enjoying things overall or I don’t want to do anything else because of it. It’s mostly that the episode kind of takes up all the attention while it’s happening.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed a real loss of interest, no. At least not in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Pretty much every time, honestly.\n\nAfterward I get really embarrassed, even if nobody saw it happen. It’s like I’m sitting there with the wrappers or whatever, and I just feel kind of… grossed out with myself. Not even only physically, although there’s some of that, but more like, Why did I do that again? Why couldn’t I just stop? And then I start picking myself apart in my head.\n\nI think part of why it bothers me so much is that in other areas I’m usually pretty disciplined. Like with school stuff, and especially robotics, I can sit there for hours and troubleshoot something or stay organized and push through even when it’s frustrating. So then when this happens, it feels really out of character, or like there’s this one part of me that I just have no control over. And that makes me feel kind of ashamed, like I should be able to handle it better.\n\nSo yeah, I definitely criticize myself after. I try not to, but that’s usually where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide evidence of your eating during these episodes, like throwing away wrappers or cleaning up quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, but maybe not always in the super obvious way of, like, immediately destroying all evidence or something. It’s more that I try to manage what people can see.\n\nLike if my roommates are around, especially if they’re in the kitchen, I’ll eat what looks like a pretty normal amount. I’ll make it seem like I’m just grabbing a snack or making a normal meal, and I’ll kind of act casual about it. But then later I’ll take extra stuff back to my room, or I’ll wait until I’m alone to get more, because I really don’t want them seeing the actual amount I’m eating.\n\nIt feels embarrassing to have someone watch that or even just notice it. I’ll think, Oh, they’re going to judge me, or they’re going to wonder why I’m eating again, or why I’m taking that much. So I end up doing this weird thing where the part people see is controlled, and then the rest happens privately.\n\nAnd yeah, sometimes I’ll clean up quickly or make sure wrappers aren’t just sitting out, but the bigger thing is honestly hiding the amount from people in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the taste of the food is pleasing to you during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Sometimes at the very beginning, maybe, but honestly during the actual episode I don’t think I’m really enjoying the taste in a normal way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m eating really fast. Like I’ll be standing at my desk or kind of hovering there between doing homework and starting a game, and I’m just putting food in my mouth without really slowing down. I’ll think I’m just having some, and then a few minutes later I look down and it’s basically gone. And I realize I barely even tasted it.\n\nSo if you asked me, “Was it good?” I could probably say yeah, it was food I like, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m savoring it or getting much pleasure from the flavor. It’s more automatic and rushed, I guess. Like the taste is almost in the background, and then afterward I’m surprised by how quickly I ate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the food tastes good to you while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… kind of, but not in the way it would if I were actually sitting down and enjoying it. Like, the food is usually stuff I like, so I’m sure it tastes good at first or on some level, but during those times I’m eating so fast that I barely register it.\n\nA lot of the time I’m standing at my desk, sort of in between things, like I’ve been doing homework and I’m about to start a game or I’m half-distracted, and I’ll just keep grabbing bites without really thinking. Then a few minutes later I look and it’s gone, and I’m like, wait, I ate all of that already? So I guess the taste is there, but it’s not really the main thing I’m noticing. It’s more rushed and automatic than actually pleasurable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a trance-like state or on autopilot when you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “autopilot” is probably the right word, or at least close to it. I don’t know if I’d call it a trance exactly, like I’m completely unaware of what’s happening, because I do know I’m eating. It’s not like I black out or anything.\n\nBut once I start eating like that, it feels like some switch gets flipped. Like there’s this momentum, and I just keep going. And the frustrating part is that I’m not totally absent in my head—I’ll actually be thinking, Dennis, just stop already. Like, you don’t need more. Put it down. Walk away. And then I just… don’t. I keep reaching for more anyway.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of why I came in, because it scares me a little how hard it feels to interrupt it once it starts. It’s like part of me is watching it happen and trying to get control back, but the eating is already moving faster than my decision-making, if that makes sense."
  }
